- (de) mr president, mr duisenberg, ladies and gentlemen, the radwan report is particularly close to my heart because it cannot be considered separately from the next item on the agenda, the information campaign on the euro. i am glad that 11 of the 12 amendments i tabled in committee have been adopted. three things were particularly important to me in these amendments. first, the strengthening and emphasising of the independence of the european central bank. in my view, this independence also implies independence from any instructions, which is justified by the fact that the ecb is bound by a clear mission, namely the maintenance of price stability. secondly, the request by parliament to the european central bank to continue its stability policy in order to prevent the rising risks of inflation. thirdly, the warning to all the applicant countries that accession to economic and monetary union does not depend on political concessions but on compliance with the convergence criteria. this report is also important for the information campaign - as has already been said - because the exaggerated importance given by the media to the external value of the euro, which has psychological repercussions on citizens' confidence in the common currency, must be contrasted more forcefully with the verifiable successes of economic and monetary union, successes which we must all make more visible. information on the technical transition should not be subordinated to information on policy objectives and successes. i therefore conclude that the euro - and the report shows this - is a project for growth and employment, for a successful internal market, for the strengthening of european identity. the euro is a project for price stability and a reduction in inflation rates, for increased competitiveness and for sound public finances, so that we can finally put an end, everywhere, to the budgetary sacrifice of our future.
the rapporteur. - (de) mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, in 500 days' time, euro notes and coins will be introduced. this will be the last stage of the first currency conversion of this magnitude in the history of our continent. all citizens of the economic and monetary union, all citizens of the member states of the european union, of the candidate countries and beyond are directly or indirectly concerned. every consumer, citizen or tourist on our continent, and the entire world economy must learn to recognize the new currency, to use the new value scale, to manipulate the new currency. large and small businesses alike need to adapt and convert their accounting and it systems to the new currency and carry out extensive preparatory work. we have had a long debate on the advantages of the euro and economic and monetary union. but beyond these considerations, we still have much, much to do. opinion polls show that the euro is still too little used by both businesses and individuals, and that 'euro-consciousness' is still very low. 30% of enterprises do not yet see the need for long-term preparation and some even believe that they will not be able to make the final switch until 2002. it is rare for a citizen to have a euro account and to carry out financial transactions in euro. the information needs and awareness-raising measures, which go beyond purely technical information related to currency conversion, are immense. the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy did not simply comment on the commission's report. we welcome the priorities and measures put forward. we have tried to give substance to the report, to go into it in greater depth and - when we considered it necessary - to complete it. given that some 80 million euros are being spent on this information campaign for the period 1999-2002, to which 80 million euros are being added by the ecb, it is absolutely essential that the closest possible coordination be carried out between the institutions, organisations and states concerned, that we establish a clear definition of priorities and that the key messages be delivered in a coherent manner, that we carry out further monitoring - to this end i propose the interinstitutional working group between the commission and parliament - that we apply policies, including in the treaties concluded with the member states, and that we finally demonstrate a high degree of professionalism in the implementation of these practical measures. let me briefly return to the minute and a half i have left on priorities. more than half of smes do not yet have an action plan for conversion. smes must start their technical conversion as soon as possible and reflect on the strategic implications of a market characterised by price transparency and increased competition. as a result, smes are the priority for autumn 2000. secondly, the general public, which includes all target groups and beyond. the citizens must be reassured. in this respect, the public administration must play a role model, as must ngos and political institutions in their role as informers and intermediaries. thirdly, schools, pupils and young people. we are proposing very concretely not only to adapt all school textbooks as quickly as possible, but also to support all painting, drawing and writing competitions as well as oral competitions on the emotional and identity theme for europe, 'the euro and me'. thirdly, we have the world savings days in the banks, which we are applying throughout europe. we must also introduce euro savings days, to strengthen the establishment of euro savings books even before the conversion phase. we must strengthen the involvement of public representatives, that is to say not only the members of the european parliament but also all other public representatives, from the municipality to the european level. next to personal investment, including in the departments of the directorates-general, coordination is the most important area, to fulfil our clearly defined mission in a broad process of communication with the citizen on the basis of sufficient financial means and time - that is why we propose to extend the campaign until 2002. secondly, the success of the euro will not end there, but will be further strengthened by the support of the citizens.

- (de) mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i was pleased to hear yesterday the speech by our new president-in-office, jacques chirac, who said that we should pay more attention than we have done so far to the precautionary principle in the field of the environment. i do not know whether jacques chirac knew the details of this report, but he has clearly indicated the way for tomorrow's vote. all phthalates in plastic toys should be banned, not just six phthalates. the scientific committee on toxicity has expressed some doubts about the most widely used phthalate and serious doubts about another. if we take our work seriously at all, then we must apply the precautionary principle and ban all phthalates as plasticizers. several of the 15 member states - and we are 15 and not 14, mr president - have applied this european precautionary principle; my home country, austria, is one of them. we have, in the framework of a decree - official gazette, decrees and orders ii no 225 of 1998 - promulgated a ban on all plasticizers in the phthalate series. i can only encourage other countries to follow this path. i do not mean bilaterally, but naturally within the framework of the 15. let me make a second point. the prohibitions are enforced only when the scientific evidence is irrefutable. my god, we should all be ashamed of ourselves now. how could we have only allowed these six phthalates to be used as plasticizers in pvc, without having previously had scientific proof that they were completely safe? when our grandchildren's health was at stake, we didn't shout for scientific proof. let's try tomorrow to fix what we started in the past.

- (de) mr president, i should like to clarify mrs kauppi's question, namely on the strategy for the level of invention. a high level of invention has the advantage that it is easily defendable in court, because actions for annulment are a serious problem. a low level of invention has the advantage that many companies and inventors will be able to file their discoveries and receive their patents very quickly. i therefore believe that we should think about the strategy that europe intends to adopt in the future, because the japanese, for example, are now applying the principle of low invention and are therefore very active in patent applications. europe and the european patent office, on the other hand, have a high level of invention. i think it is of enormous importance as a political and economic instrument.

(cheers and applause) i think that's what we need in the future: learning has to be fun. in life, the learning pyramid is actually reversed. when it comes to learning, we used to think of young people as full-time students, whereas today we find that working people need to learn more and more. i think that we in the european parliament can confirm that we actually learn, evaluate and judge more every day than we had to do in a study or in school. also, as you get older, you become more aware of what you want to learn. you become much more selective, and it's the joy of learning that makes you excited and that makes you want to take in new things. so we have a paradigm shift, not just in the economy, but in a broad range of social strata, not just through e-learning, through the new technological possibilities, but also through a change in consciousness. i think this shift in consciousness is actually showing that adults in all walks of life are actually seeing this lifelong learning as more and more important. i therefore think it is very good that we in the european parliament are dealing with such longterm strategic issues. on a friday like today, i think it is important that we have the courage to think strategically in the long term about how best to support the citizens of europe. the issue of health until old age is becoming increasingly important, especially for older people. we have much to learn about how to eat properly, how to take care of our bodies properly. i think these very basic virtues are very important for the future. what do we need now at european community level? we need a general system of accounting for private, public and company education, a european education passport, a european driving licence, where you can use comparable and europe-wide educational building blocks. we need comparisons of education expenditure and income and a comparison of skill levels across europe. we should try to introduce best practice worldwide, an incentive for positive competition. we should learn from the world what system, what education is best for our people and citizens in europe. (applause from the centre)
- mr president, i have to go. lifelong learning can be a task not only of the european commission and the european institutions, but also and above all of the member states. i voted yes because i believe that tax incentives for vocational and private education must be put at the centre, so that education sponsorship is also possible. incentives must be created for foundations to take over educational institutions and for broad-based support to be provided to relieve the system. but i also believe that we should be an example at european level. we should design and evaluate training and continuing training plans for our civil servants and politicians every year, so that we can measure how we are progressing ourselves.
mr president, we are indeed in the third oil crisis. but there is a major difference between the two oil crises. these were political crises that were manufactured, 1973 and 1979 with the fall of the shah's regime. for 15 years now, there has been clear evidence and studies - such as global 2000, the report to jimmy carter - that there will be a real shortage of raw materials at the turn of the millennium. that would mean that the third oil crisis will be a permanent one. it has nothing to do with the possibility that the price will go down again in the short term, but it will go up permanently in the long term. also, the study by colin j. campbell - whoever wants it, can get it from me - says that in the first years of the 21st century, the raw materials - oil - will become scarce, because the production curve has crossed the peak. that's why i think even demonstrations will not help, because you can't squeeze out more juice from a lemon than there is in it. we'll have oil, but not on the cheap terms. that is, the price of oil will rise to $40 to $60 in the future. there are enough studies to support this. even if they are wrong, we must now consider and analyze them. even a tax cut would not change the fact that oil prices are still rising. it is not an opec crisis either, because only 40% of the oil produced comes from opec, the other 60% comes from non-opec countries. if it were only a crisis that was man-made, it would be offset by the other oil producers.
i have to say that i am not in favour of this. the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area. almost to the day we can celebrate a ten-year anniversary. almost exactly ten years ago, the debate began on which country should actually be responsible for handling asylum procedures. the dublin agreement was signed. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. we've been talking for years about the need for such a tool. but we have not succeeded so far, and tomorrow we have a unique opportunity to actually implement eurodac. i'd like that. this would also be welcomed by many of the council presidents who have now taken office, and each one has promised that he will make it a top priority to try to make eurodac a reality. it is a long story that we have before us, and it is about an important point. why do we need eurodac? what can eurodac do? eurodac is a system for comparing fingerprints, which is useful for determining which country was the first country of contact for the refugee and which country is therefore responsible for the asylum procedure, which also results in burden sharing among european countries. and it is, of course, also a tool to determine whether an applicant has previously applied for asylum. i would like to say that the commission is very much aware of the problems which have arisen in the past, and that it is not the commission's intention to make a distinction between the two. it has a long history, as i said, and was last discussed in parliament in december 1999. we have agreed on a number of points, firstly, that eurodac must be implemented and, secondly, on the conditions under which it must be implemented. we are in agreement with commissioner vitorino, in particular, and i would also like to thank him for doing everything he could to make eurodac a reality. we have here established that the implementing powers are vested in the commission in accordance with the treaty. the council met in december immediately after our decision and, in a completely incomprehensible fashion, reversed these proposals, which had come from the commission and parliament, and assumed powers in the most important areas. this has led to a re-consultation of parliament, which means that we have to deal with it again, but only on the points which relate to it. that is articles 22 and 23 and nothing more. we have also confined ourselves to this in the committee, except for mrs boumediene-thiery, who has opened a new debate. i don't think she's getting the point. the only thing that was discussed was articles 22 and 23. the new proposal provides that the council will want to retain the full provision in the main areas of the implementing rules: where it concerns the collection, transmission and comparison of fingerprints, where it concerns the blocking of data on asylum seekers and where it concerns the compilation of statistics on asylum seekers. we have unanimously agreed in committee to follow our old proposal and reject what the council has proposed. why would you do that? for two very important reasons. the first reason is: this approach is contrary to article 202 of the ec treaty, which lays down the principle that the commission is to be entrusted with the implementation of the rules adopted by the council. that's important in this case. the commission can react and implement this scheme with much more independence - that is, independence from various national interests - and objectivity. that is why we as a parliament support it. we are also against the council's proposal for a second reason. it is against parliament because it excludes parliament, because the comitology procedure no longer exists and because we no longer have the necessary information and are not involved. this proposal would curtail and diminish the powers of parliament. for these two very good reasons we are in favour of what the commission and commissioner vitorino have proposed, namely that the commission be given the task of implementing it, and i ask you all to be present for the vote tomorrow. we are taking a step towards greater security for europe, a step against abuse. take this opportunity and make eurodac a reality at last!
i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. i would like to say first of all that my delegation will not refuse to approve the skinner report on the second amendment to the directive on minimum health and safety requirements. the health of workers is a matter of concern to all of us and is at the heart of our concerns. no one wants to be accused of being anti-social, and there is no doubt that minimum standards in the european area are justified. it is also in line with the eu treaty, and we are therefore paying due respect to the rapporteur. it is not clear why meps are always faced with the dilemma of being accused of anti-social behaviour or of being laughed at. it is not easy to imagine that the adult citizen should be told how to climb or descend a ladder, that a risk assessment should be carried out before using a ladder, that two people should not climb a ladder at the same time, or that the time spent on a ladder should be limited to thirty minutes or a special finesse to 25% of the total working time. the skinner report will go down in the history of this house as a flagship report, and the citizens of the european union will rightly ask themselves whether we have nothing better to do than to take for granted certain things which are really a matter for the house and have no place in an eu directive or in a related report by the parliament which is involved in the decision-making process! the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. i should like to begin by thanking the rapporteur, mr evans, very warmly for his report and also for his presentation, which already puts this report and the commission's communication in the larger context of the necessary reform of competition policy. we are aware of the pace of change in the world economy and therefore of the growing importance of research and development, the importance of cooperation between firms and the need for specialisation of firms and therefore of specialisation agreements. for us, competition is not an end in itself, but a means to an end for growth and employment, and those who, like the commissioner and us, are in favour of a functioning internal market in the sense of the social market economy model of regulation are not in favour of unlimited competition, but of competition within the framework of the necessary regulatory principles, with responsibility also for the future and for fellow men. it has already been said today that the reform is very welcome and that cooperation between companies is necessary, that the communication gives companies greater freedom in their cooperation agreements and that it leads to simplification, flexibility and liberalisation. we have that on the side of the barn. we also have criticisms and wishes. the rapporteur has cut some of them; i want to go into it again. firstly, commissioner, we believe that people are longing for legal certainty in the face of increasing self-responsibility. i therefore wonder whether the different treatment of horizontal and vertical restrictions really leads to more legal certainty or whether a block exemption regulation would not be better for both. secondly, we are asking that the results be used not for five years but for ten years. we urge you to make it clear, by including it in the guideline, that reciprocal or unilateral subcontracting agreements - collective supply in short - are not affected by the prohibition in article 81 (2) and we also believe - as stated in the report of the committee on economic and monetary affairs - that you should reconsider the definition of market share. what market are we talking about? the buying market, the selling market, the national market, the regional market? we all know that the market for the firms concerned is constantly changing and we also express the fear that guidelines 122 and 141 will set an arbitrary market limit of 15%. we believe that an extension of this market value is necessary, just as the transitional arrangements should be extended for at least two years to achieve their full purpose.
mr president, this draft regulation is one of the first to be adopted by codecision in the field of development policy. the european parliament has lived up to its greater responsibility, which it has by means of more decision-making power. many have said that this regulation must be completed as soon as possible and that the european parliament must therefore abstain from many of its amendments. the path we have chosen was undoubtedly the right one. we have been able to achieve some improvements to this proposal for a regulation. one of the most important improvements - as the rapporteur has already mentioned - is the adequate financing of the possible activities provided for by this regulation. the increase in appropriations from eur 50.4 million to eur 93 million, which is in fact a doubling, is a great success, even though we know that a much higher amount would be necessary given the importance of this subject for the future of us all. but this leads to a more general and political debate which, of course, goes beyond the scope of this regulation. the amendments we have tabled have enabled us to achieve a number of changes in article 3, which deals with the type of measures to be implemented. i am sorry that the european parliament's amendment, which calls for the elimination of bank guarantees or credit guarantees where necessary in order to widen the range of applicants and the target group as much as possible, was not adopted. i believe that this is a great success for parliament and for the rapporteur.

mr president, i should like to say a word about mr prodi, the president of the commission. i can only say, in response to your speech, that it is time. i, like many here, have waited a long time for this clear word. mr moscovici, the european parliament has from the outset called for the charter of fundamental rights to be incorporated into the treaties, made legally binding and for people to have access to the european court of justice if it is violated. this demand is not some political position. it is a necessary consequence of the nature of the matter, of the nature of fundamental rights, and it corresponds to the expectations and demands of the people. can we seriously proclaim rights without establishing them as rights? can you seriously proclaim rights and at the same time refuse to make them your own law? can we recognize fundamental rights but deny people the tools and means to defend them? each article of this charter is already enshrined in international conventions or in the constitutions of the member states or in international law or in eu treaties and protocols. they're nothing new. they represent the sum of 200 years of fundamental rights tradition in europe. the only question to be answered is whether the european union and its institutions are as committed to this european tradition of fundamental rights as the member states. can it really be, mr moscovici, that governments are afraid to make long-established rights into valid, enforceable, internal law of the european union? the european council and the member states' governments would then admit to the world that for decades all these international conventions have been regarded as political confessionals without any legal weight. would it not then arouse suspicion among the people that the european council and the governments of the member states are trying to save the area of intergovernmental cooperation from the charter of fundamental rights and to protect their own power and their own absolute power from the principles of the rule of law, judicial controls and fundamental legal orders? that's the suspect in the room! mr moscovici, it is a little bizarre that heads of state and government want to proclaim the right of defence against the state. it is up to parliaments to proclaim fundamental rights and to enshrine them as rights. but i think that the governments and the european council are well advised to regard parliament's vote in favour of binding legislation and incorporation into the treaties as a very important argument in this situation and to bow to this demand. (applause from the centre)

the rapporteur. the commission has already done so. i am speaking in my dual capacity as rapporteur for malta and as chairman of the joint parliamentary committee with poland. i am therefore grateful for the extension of the speaking time. due to its geopolitical location as europe's outermost outpost at the interface with the middle east and north africa, malta's importance extends far beyond its geographical size and small population. the european parliament and the vienna european council, with wolfgang schüssel, then austrian foreign minister, holding the presidency of the council, therefore welcomed malta's decision to reactivate its application for membership, which had been frozen since 1996. it was only logical that the helsinki european council last december, by extending the accession negotiations, also gave the green light to the resumption of accession negotiations with malta. there is no doubt that malta fulfils the copenhagen political criteria and, as a functioning market economy, should be able to withstand competitive pressure within the union if it continues the measures currently under way to restructure its industry and reduce its budget deficit. malta also has stable, functioning democratic institutions. the national action plan for the implementation of the acquis creates the necessary conditions for structural change and the modernisation of the island state's economy. as rapporteur, i am of course aware that malta has similar problems with eu adjustment as other candidate countries, only to a much lesser extent and therefore easier for the eu to cope with. the small island has, of course, been accustomed for a long time to protecting its economy from too much competition by means of protectionist measures and tariffs. the breakdown of these structures therefore naturally raises concerns among small retailers and family businesses. it also creates fear in the shipyard and dry dock sector, which has been heavily subsidised for a long time and therefore contributes to the national debt. but reforms are under way which can contribute to the economic viability and survival of this sector. malta is also well on its way to opening up its monopoly of electronic media to the free market. one problem is undoubtedly the great need to catch up in the field of environmental policy and waste disposal. these are problems which must be overcome within a reasonable timeframe. the financial support under the pre-accession strategy and the awareness that financial assistance to malta should be in line with the rules applicable to the other candidate countries are essential for malta. the main problem in malta, however, is that the accession process is still viewed differently by the government and the opposition, and the european union is keen to ensure that this process is continuous. therefore, comprehensive public information on the political, economic and social aspects of malta's accession to the european union and a comprehensive dialogue with all sections of the population and politically relevant groups and parties is needed to make malta's accession a national concern and to continuously shape malta's path to the eu. i now turn to poland, which is undoubtedly a key country in the process of enlargement, not only because it is the most expansive of all the central european economies, but also because of its size and geopolitical position. poland has always been the first victim of european conflicts which, until recently, were fought on its soil. it is not his fault that it is so late to find its way into the european union. the commission has already taken a number of initiatives to improve the quality of the information provided by the member states. it cannot be measured by mere numbers, such as cost-benefit calculations. especially on this german unity day, it is worth remembering that it was the polish solidarity movement that 20 years ago initiated the process of the collapse of the soviet power bloc and ultimately made german reunification possible. it is essential to keep in mind two elements: firstly, the eu must complete its internal reforms in order to be ready to receive from 2003. it is also essential to provide the polish public with comprehensive information on the advantages and opportunities of accession. as difficult as the negotiations between poland and the eu may be, we as representatives of the european parliament are called upon to do everything possible to give credibility to poland's integration process. it is not a matter of the exact year. but there can be no doubt about the european union's will to extend its zone of peace and stability to central europe and poland. the tendency of the report on poland to encourage its politicians to speed up the legislative alignment with the eu and to implement the acquis, as well as the successful conclusion of the agreement on the liberalization of polish agricultural trade a few days ago, are positive signs in this respect. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.

draft opinion of the consultative committee on regional policy, transport and tourism. the commission is not let me begin with a preliminary remark, not on the subject of austria, but on another political subject. before reporting on the two candidate countries, cyprus and malta, the french presidency has changed its political presence in this house. i hope this is not an indication of how the great nation wants to treat its small partners, it would be the wrong signal. but now for the real signals. elmar brok rightly began his speech by pointing out that we will all be the beneficiaries of enlargement, especially those of us on this side of the current external border. commissioner verheugen, you rightly stressed that it is no longer a question of whether, but of how and when enlargement will take place. but it is precisely in this 'how' and 'when' that we all need to ensure that enlargement is carefully prepared and seriously implemented so that it can be a success. the committee on regional policy, transport and tourism has tried to take into account in its areas of competence two sub-areas which were raised by mr brok and which we must take into account if enlargement is to be a success. we have just heard from the regional policy and promotion sector something on the subject of sugar-bread. transport and tourism fall into the category of the whip, because the point is that we have a very large amount of acquis in this area, and the point is - i will come back to this again - to actually apply this acquis. but first, a reminder that transport infrastructure will be central to the success of enlargement. a large single market with over 500 million european consumers needs transport routes to make this market work. the infrastructure for this is partly in place, at least in half of the future union. in this area, we have developed or are in the process of improving the tens, which are essential infrastructure measures. we must do the same, and in particular we must organize the connection for central and eastern europe. the tina report gives the appropriate perspective. the financial perspective provides for some of the funds. the question is whether this will be enough, and the candidate countries have a sound basis in phare, ispa and other financial programmes. but there must be a sound basis, and it is particularly important that the infrastructure be created in the candidate countries to ensure that acceptance of the acquis communautaire is not just a paper matter. the committee has tried to deal with the most important aspects of each mode of transport in a very simple way. in particular, two very important aspects of road and rail transport are to be noted here: in the case of road transport, which is already largely liberalised and open, it is necessary to ensure that, if the member states accede, the community rules on labour, social, taxation and excise duties are actually applied in each of them, so that, especially if they do not, there are no market disturbances in the european union and in the acceding countries which we may then have to remedy with great effort. similar early action is and will be necessary to align and integrate the western and eastern european rail networks. we must ensure that what is already rail infrastructure in central and eastern europe is not replaced by road transport and then tried hard to put things back on track. inland waterway transport will be important for environmental reasons, because we need to get bulk goods off the road, and if we can't transport them by rail, then we have to use waterways. with the accession of malta and cyprus, the union will have the largest merchant fleet in the world. it is very important that this should not be a flag of convenience but that all the measures required by the acquis should be applied in order to give priority to safety. we can't have another case like erika. there are a number of other points which the committee of the regions has noted in its report. i hope that the candidate countries and ourselves take them to heart.

mr president, the recognition that the fundamental rights of man are not a mere promise, but an inalienable right of every person, not granted by politics and the state, but recognized, derived from the dignity of man, the knowledge that rights are only effective if they are the law in force, guaranteed by parliament in laws, by courts. this knowledge is the very idea of modern democracy, and it is at the heart of all the constitutions of the member countries. the european council is violating this basic consensus of europe by refusing to incorporate the charter of fundamental rights into the treaties, to make it legally binding and to give citizens whose rights have been violated access to the european court of justice. fundamental rights are essentially rights of defence against the state. it is not strange that it is the heads of state or government who are supposed to be deciding on the fundamental rights of people in europe. it is not surprising that these heads of government refuse to submit to the charter's fundamental rights regime and make it legally binding. the council's claim to power is the biggest problem in the actual constitutional process and is the root of the union's democratic deficit. the heads of state or government want to be not only the de facto sole legislator of the union, but also the government. who still doubts that they see themselves as the sole constituent of the union? the european parliament is the only institution in the world that has the power to make decisions. it has always been the task of democracy to tame the power of the prince. we must ask ourselves today whether we want a europe of princes or a european democracy. if we want european democracy, we must initiate a constitutional process and lay the foundations for it with the charter of fundamental rights. currencies. it sent letters of complaint to nearly 120 banks and banking groups. the banks were given until the beginning of october to respond, but the commission made it clear that it would punish very severely price agreements on conversion charges, as it considers that they are detrimental to the introduction of euro coins and banknotes and to consumers. the exchange rates were irrevocably standards (ecbs), which allow for direct and automatic transmission of cross-border payments. a survey of the 11 euro area countries showed that the average exchange fee for a 100 euro transfer was mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your work. our aim is and must be to turn the economic union into a security union. this is a mandate which arises from the treaty of amsterdam and the tampere summit. but this is also a necessity in order to strengthen the european union as an economic centre, and it is also a wish of the citizens which we must take into account, because the citizens of the european union have a right to security. today we are discussing how to take a step forward towards this security union. we are discussing this in the light of the measures on money laundering. if money is the engine of organized crime, then the measures to combat global money laundering that we are discussing today are part of our fight against this organized crime. if we can prevent money laundering, then we can also prevent organized crime. the organized crime - this has been done here several times, and we know this - makes huge profits from drug crime, human trafficking, proliferation, car smuggling, from many business sectors to the art trade. all this illegally earned money is laundered into the legal economy. this is also the aim of organised crime, and it is in this way that organised crime and money laundering damage the legal economy of the european union. the economy is being subverted dramatically. this is why these measures to combat money laundering are so important. the only way to tackle this is to combine the different instruments to combat money laundering. we have the opportunity today to discuss four such reports and measures. the first is to increase the powers of europol. we know that europol has powers, but now money laundering is added as a separate area of competence. that's good. but we must not forget to provide europol with enough staff and enough money so that europol can then actually exercise those powers which we are transferring to europol. i would also like to take this opportunity to ask, as my colleagues have done before, that the serious anomaly be finally removed and that europol be given the same scrutiny as other institutions of the european union. the second measure, the second instrument, is harmonization. europol, with its corresponding powers, will only make sense if we also succeed in bringing about harmonisation in the field of substantive and formal criminal law provisions, when it comes to the criminal record, when it comes to the extent of the penalty or the recovery of the proceeds of crime. the third point is a very important one, namely that there will also be improvements in legal aid. mrs palacio has emphasized the need to involve the relevant professional groups and to make it clear to the member states that european interests must be put above national interests. the last point for me - and i include this - is the establishment of eurojust or the interim unit, because the aim is to achieve better coordination in the investigation and prosecution of crimes. the european union, we, the european parliament, are seizing a new opportunity to combat this organized crime which is so important to us. the union is thus building a bridge from economic union to security union.

the draft european charter of fundamental rights is now available. we have only to say yes or no to this today, and the rapporteurs propose that the plenary of this house adopt and approve this charter of fundamental rights. we do this with conviction. i know that many of you and i regret that we are only saying yes or no today and are not able to send the council the other political messages that might have been necessary in this context and that would express the critical support of this house for the charter of fundamental rights, which can only be a first step because it is still a long way from what parliament asked for this political project in march of this year. but it is also necessary to recall here what the value of this charter will be if it is adopted. i believe there is a first fundamental right, namely an unwritten fundamental right, the right of people to know their rights. this fundamental right is now being fulfilled. the european union's vague formulations and legal principles are being replaced by a clear system of fundamental rights, binding on all eu institutions and on any application of european law by the member states. the value of this charter is also to make enshrined rights in international law - enshrined but not binding, enshrined but not enforceable, enshrined but without guarantees - legal principles, indeed constitutional principles of the union itself, and there will certainly be a dynamic developing here that will one day lead to the legal binding of this charter of fundamental rights. it is a revolution in the debate and in the history of fundamental and human rights that this charter, for the first time, includes social rights on an equal footing with classical human rights and classical principles, as is not the case in any catalogue of fundamental rights in a member state. this charter also expresses a common basis of values for all the candidate countries, for all the countries which want to join this european union, and, particularly in the field of social rights, it is a clear signal to the candidate countries to meet these social standards. it means, even if some heads of state and government do not want to accept it, i am deeply convinced that it also means the opening of the constitutional process which this parliament has so often and so tenaciously called for. i am convinced that this charter of fundamental rights has laid the foundations for a future european constitution and constitutional process. i think that can move us all to agree to this charter, even though it may be difficult to recognize or accept in detail that many rights, some essential rights which parliament has rightly and for good reason called for for the people of europe, are not included in this charter. it is not to be seen that the right to a fair wage is not included, that we have encountered insurmountable resistance when it came to the inclusion of the right to a decent minimum standard of living, the right to work, the right to housing. i think that this charter must also be completed in the future if it is to become the cornerstone of a european constitution. but that cannot prevent us from taking that first step with determination. i hope that this parliament, even if it only says yes to this charter of fundamental rights today, will not let any doubt arise in the coming weeks that it is demanding that the council make this charter legally binding and that this charter must also open up access to the european court of justice for the people. i hope that this house will emphasize these demands by a large majority in its resolution on nice. i'll finish. i would just like to thank my co-rapporteur, if i may, for a great, dedicated and admirable job, and i have to say, madam president, i've been a champion of the bill of rights from the beginning. i have always been in favour of its being recognised as legally binding. but that does not change the fact that i cannot agree with the prevailing euphoric assessment of the convention as an instrument of future constitutional developments. apart from the problems of substance of the charter which we have already discussed today, the mere referral of national parliamentarians is no substitute for a thorough involvement of national parliaments in matters of constitutional importance. i would like to draw attention to the fact that the convention is not a mere instrument of the european union. as a former member of the supreme court, i can assure you that the overlapping jurisdiction of different supreme courts will lead to divergent jurisprudence in the very short term, and that divergent jurisprudence is to be expected. we must not lose sight of this problem.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter. power is about control. the european institutions have power, and we must limit that power in the service of the citizens. it is high time to close the gap in the protection of fundamental rights in europe that has existed so far. for many years there has been debate as to whether the union should not simply accede to the european convention on human rights as do the member states themselves. i am glad that for a number of reasons the union has not acceded to the convention on human rights. in view of the text of the charter which we are going to approve today - and i hope by a large majority - it is clear that with our charter we will achieve more and more up-to-date protection of fundamental rights than would have been achieved by accession to the european convention on human rights. only a third of the charter repeats the guarantees of the human rights convention. the vast majority of the remainder are concerned with the political rights of the citizens of the union, with economic and social rights and, last but not least, with the important questions of the future, such as the environment, health and consumer protection. the charter must become european law if it is to fulfil its function. the step we are taking today is just one step on a long road ahead. the solemn declaration must become binding european constitutional law. the commission has already taken a number of initiatives in this area, and i am sure that the commission will take them up. i'm not going to. the demand for fundamental rights in the union, combined with the desire to constitutionalise the treaties, does not mean that the united states will be the end of the road. but we need as soon as possible a constitutional text for europe in which the citizens can find clear and comprehensible statements about their europe, what rights the citizens themselves have, where the union has assumed responsibility and where the responsibilities should also be exercised in the future by the member states and how europe will fulfil its tasks with all the guarantees of the protection of human rights under the rule of law. we must continue to work on this task! let's go this way!

the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. let me say at the outset that i welcome in principle the commission's proposal to set up a compensation fund, also in the interests of austrian agriculture. this fund can be a suitable instrument to reduce losses of pig farmers in times of crisis. the pig crisis of 1998 and 1999 in particular showed that the instruments of the present common organization of the market in pigmeat, such as private storage and export refunds, are not sufficient. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the commission has not, however, included cofinancing in its proposal. i believe that the basic principle of eu funding in the context of common market organisations should also apply to gmo pigmeat. this would ensure intensive participation by farmers and thus indirectly limit production. the proposal provides for a body to be set up by the member states. i think it is important to ensure that all member states set up this fund so that farmers throughout europe have the same opportunity to participate. as far as the management of this fund is concerned, the state administration should be reduced to a minimum, i.e. the member states should approve and review the funds but not administer them themselves, leaving this to the producer groups, for example. the participation in this compensation fund should be open to all farmers. but if he does, he should commit to five years. i am convinced that this instrument will bring greater stability to the market and will enable farmers in europe to cope better with cyclical crises.

adopted by the commission on 16 december. the commission is not we adopted the directive on passenger cable cars in march of this year. if the directive had been implemented, the accident in austria might not have happened. it is clear that legislation has a great responsibility here and it would be desirable if the directives were implemented as quickly as possible, particularly in such sensitive areas as product safety. i hope that will happen before 3 may 2002. the present product safety regulation is a general directive, which mainly covers those areas not covered by existing directives. i would like to thank the commission for adding a very important new point here, and that is the presumption of conformity, which means that if products meet standards in europe, they are considered safe. i believe that this principle is of enormous importance for consumer protection in particular, because it allows you to check at any time whether a product meets the standards or not. in this respect, amendment no 37 - namely that external certification is carried out and that ce declarations are not only issued by traders but are verified by external neutral bodies - is of particular importance for consumer protection, because it will of course significantly increase consumer confidence in such declarations. i have a problem with amendment no 14 on the publication of standards in the official journal, of course, because the current copyright is owned by the national standards bodies and therefore the european union would have to pay royalties. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i therefore have very serious reservations and would like to reject this provision. on the 'export ban' point, we cannot automatically assume that our standards are the ideal ones worldwide. there are many countries which have their own regulations and safety standards which are much better suited to their purpose in those countries than our current standards in europe.
the commission has already done so. mrs gonzález álvarez has produced a very coherent report, but my group does not see the inclusion of services in product liability, as the commission has already envisaged. for example, in many industries today, products are no longer assembled by the manufacturer but are delivered to the retailer in pieces. the manufacturer who is liable is now anyone in the distribution chain who can influence the safety performance of the product, and this would mean that all dealers who are still carrying out assembly work would be considered to be manufacturers. we believe that in these cases trade should not be burdened with the manufacturer's obligations under the product safety act. there are other civil remedies, such as compensation for damage resulting from contractual liability, which certainly provide sufficient protection for consumers. i am surprised that the commission's experts have not solved this problem on their own, and i trust the new commissioner will reconsider this problem. i hope that there will be no difficulty with the commission in exempting charities and non-profit organizations from the provisions of this product safety directive. the commission has decided to initiate the procedure laid down in article 93 (2) of the eec treaty in respect of the export of certain products originating in the community. agreed, not there! but the commission's request for a safety inspection cannot be a reason to close down entire export branches. as my colleagues have said before me, at least the proof that the exporting country has different safety regulations should be accepted. i can only urge the commission to reconsider this area of concern. we're only at the first reading. we need the famous 314 votes in a second or third reading, and we certainly will not get them in the present state of the report.

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the european parliament has been a great help to us. it is good to hear socially responsible people from all the political groups here, who are ready to find a common consensus. i thank the rapporteur, and i thank you, mr michel, for your initiative in bringing the problem of supplementary sickness insurance to the fore now and in good time. i have some general remarks to make. we've never had it so good. and we can see that in the fact that our life expectancy is increasing by three years every 10 years, and it's not because we're living healthier. we're getting older because medical knowledge doubles every five years. medical care is costing more and more money! but equal access to health care must be possible for all without exception. this is what counts in a social europe, and this is what distinguishes us from others. the united states, for example, is often used as an example of a free market economy. it would be a fatal mistake in health policy to take them as our example. the us system, for example, shows us that health care costs, measured in terms of gross national product, are almost twice as high as the average for the european union and that in the united states, 40 million americans still have no or insufficient insurance cover. this has nothing to do with sound social policy. the european union therefore wants to develop health and supplementary insurance in a way that does not compromise quality and excludes the most vulnerable. the health insurance debate in the eu must not end in a dead end. until the requirements for supplementary insurance are defined at european level, we must be extremely cautious with the existing systems. the risk that citizens who are not in good health and the elderly may only be able to take out private insurance because of their high risk is not sustainable for the development of society. such errors of reasoning have no place in the european union. the first stage is therefore necessary and useful for us to draw up as soon as possible a green paper on the survey of supplementary health insurance benefits in europe. it will then be our task to define minimum standards of supplementary insurance for europe, provided we want to achieve and develop a fairly equivalent, socially balanced health care in the eu and thus speak the word of a social union. i think that's what we want, don't you?
the commission has already made a number of proposals. i would first of all like to thank my colleagues marinos, pronk and menrad, and also my colleague ettl, very warmly for their support in implementing my amendments and changing the objective of this report. the financing of statutory health care and the financing of pension schemes is at the heart of the social and budgetary debate in europe. both have similar causes, demographic change on the one hand and technical and technological advances in medicine on the other. both are based on a three-pillar model: the public, the corporate and the private. we all know that these systems are different in their size and networking and that they have different levels of performance from one member state to another. both systems also have national provisions which hinder the mobility and flexibility of workers in europe and increase costs for the employer. i am therefore glad that the rapporteur has been willing to move from his imperative call for a directive to a thorough analysis in the form of a green paper. i am glad that we have moved away from the french model of 1:1 for europe, which is not possible in this case. we need a definition of what we mean by supplementary insurance. i mean, supplementary insurance means voluntary, private or company and supplementary to the basic state insurance ... mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. transparency ensures better participation of citizens in the decision-making process and greater legitimacy, efficiency and accountability of the administration in a democratic system. the essential basis of this system must be the widest possible access to the union's documents for citizens. the regulation is in principle to be welcomed, in particular the clarification that it covers all union policies as well as all union institutions. but a large drop of wormwood remains. the list of exceptions in article 4 may, if applied extensively, completely defeat the objective of this regulation. the commission has also proposed that the court of justice should be consulted on the interpretation of the treaty. i think it is highly questionable to refuse access to documents when, to quote article 4, 'the proper functioning of the institutions could be jeopardised'. this provision is suitable for opening doors and gates arbitrarily. the concept of 'proper functioning of the institutions' is an indefinite concept which is not in keeping with the case-law of the court of justice. the court of justice considered that the assessment of whether access to a document is granted must take account of the interests of the institution, the confidentiality of the document and the interest of the applicant. the interference with the proper functioning of the institutions is not sufficient to justify a refusal of access to documents. this provision must be deleted or at least supplemented by the obligation to weigh up the interests involved. the aim of this regulation is to optimise access to documents, while respecting the principle of openness to the greatest possible extent. the proposal for a regulation can only achieve this objective if the list of exceptions in article 4 is at least relaxed.

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the european parliament has been a great help to us in the last few years. the tax systems of the union are becoming toothless and less productive. first, control systems are now so complex that even the experts are overwhelmed by the task of handling them. there are too many and too complex individual taxes. the citizens feel that the state is playing tricks on them because the experts are already overworked and cannot keep up with the tax burden. but burdens that the citizen no longer understands are automatically perceived by him as unjust. the reaction is logical, the tax is refused. the taxpayer does not necessarily have to withhold the tax. very often, personal performance is reduced, but activities have also been moved abroad. it cannot therefore be our aim to turn this negative national spiral upwards by adopting new taxes, so-called european taxes. we must be clear that the willingness of our citizens to perform will only improve if performance and work in europe are worthwhile again. we know our basic figures: with a tax rate of more than 45%, many of our member states run budget deficits. the us baseline is different. the united states has a budget surplus of several hundred billion dollars if the tax rate is less than 30%. the aim must therefore be to simplify and streamline our tax systems and to reduce tax rates rather than increase them. the taxation of aviation fuels is therefore to be rejected. the sixth vat directive needs to be simplified, if at all, by setting a ceiling on the rates of taxation, and not a lower limit. this means that the provision of electronic services must also be subject to a zero rate of taxation.
